<note>original: "Grahalaghava" (The Lightness of the Planets), "Jyotisham" (Astronomy/Astrology), "Patrani" (Leaves/Folios), "Sampurnam" (Complete), "Kartta" (Author/Creator), "Ganesha Daivajna" (Ganesha the Astrologer), "Samvat" (Era/Year).</note>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical instructions for calculating planetary positions, specifically focusing on the transition from time units to arc measurements (degrees and minutes).</meta>

# Calculation of the Elapsed Portions of the Constellations

Place the elapsed time of the current constellation <note>original: "bhuktanadi," the time already passed in a lunar mansion</note> in two places. Multiply it by three and one <note>original: "ramakubhih," using the numerical code where Rama=3 and Ku=1, meaning 13</note>; the result is the parallax. These are the minutes of the arc according to the scriptures for the elapsed time. (1)

Divide the result in two places; multiply by twenty <note>original: "nakhai," meaning 'fingernails,' a traditional code for 20</note>. The resulting quotient gives the minutes and seconds of the arc. The sum of these two becomes the clear position; the remainder is the fraction. (2)

The number of constellations starting from Ashvini <note>the first sign of the zodiac</note> that have been passed should be multiplied by sixty. Add the remaining time of the current constellation and divide the total by two. (3)

Multiply the elapsed hours of the Sun's entry into a sign <note>term: Sankranti (the moment the sun enters a new zodiac sign)</note> by the daily motion and divide by sixty <note>original: "khashat," code for 60</note>. Add the resulting degrees and minutes to the previous position to find the true position of the Sun <note>original: "Bhaskara," a name for the Sun</note>. (4)

The distances for your capital city <note>likely referring to the observer's latitude or local coordinates</note> are: 23, 31, 39, 20, 22, 34, 30, 53, and 70. <note>These numbers represent specific astronomical constants used for local calculations</note>. (1)

For Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, and Saturn, use their own high motions and the mean motion of the Sun. For Mercury and Venus, use their own fast motions <note>term: Shighrocha (the apex of quick motion/perigee)</note>; the mean motion of the Sun is considered their mean motion. (2)

->From the Grahalaghava, page 5-21<-

१ = The illustrious Keshavarka, known among astronomers, provides this awakening for the enlightenment of beginners <gloss>or of the Sun</gloss>. This work clarifies the movements of the planets and their positions through a system of logic.
The calculation of the planets is made possible through the "Bow Instrument" <note>A reference to the *Dhanur-yantra*, an astronomical instrument used for measuring angles</note> of Shiva, which simplifies the complex mathematics of sines and arcs. By immersing oneself in this method, one achieves the same results as the complex sine-based calculations, making the five-fold planetary actions <note>The *Pancha-karma*: likely referring to the five elements of the almanac or specific planetary motions</note> accessible. २
The results are fruitful and provide the means to calculate the five planets starting from the first month. ३ The shadow of the gnomon <note>The vertical part of a sun-dial</note> reveals the power of the Sun and the measure of human time. ३
Through the various forms of the Supreme Lord, Keshavarka has established these six divisions... ५

## || Salutations to the Illustrious Ganesha || The Awakening of Light, which purifies the mind and the essence of ritual action <gloss>for the benefit of all in this dark age</gloss> <gloss>the sequence of forms known by the wise</gloss> <gloss>Knowledge 1</gloss> <gloss>2</gloss> <gloss>3</gloss> <gloss>4</gloss> <gloss>5</gloss> <gloss>6</gloss>
### Full of meaning. Though composed of few letters, it reveals the form of the country; victory to the teaching of Keshava. || १ || 

<margin>
Where the thread of the formula is brief, through that door, the measurement is found on another page. Produced by one's own effort, the collection of days is obtained. १
The instrument given by the one named Bankala... the center of the moon... the seven sages... the base lines and others are to be known by the name given. ३
</margin>

The arc, equal to the sine, is held by the multiplier of qualities; the well-rounded arm brings forth the fruit, providing great auspiciousness.

# And do change. || २ || Although many teachers have created treasuries of calculation, they shine through the means of the "Bow" <gloss>12 ||</gloss>
I am now ready to clearly explain the planetary section, making it very light and easy, devoid of the difficult labor of sines and arcs. || ३ ||

# Now, the calculation of the Ahargana <note>The total count of civil days elapsed from a specific epoch to a given date</note>
### is stated ||

<margin>
Through the baths, gifts, and prayers spoken by the Brahmins, the actions of Keshava and others are performed. १
</margin>

Subtract the Shaka era <gloss>1442</gloss> <note>This corresponds to 1520 CE, providing a date for the text</note> from the current year. Multiply by the number of months and divide the remainder... starting with the month of Chaitra, add them together. Multiply the cycle by two... add the intercalary months <term>Adhimasa</term> <note>Extra months added to the lunar calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar year</note>. || ४ || <gloss>4</gloss> Multiply by sixty-three <gloss>63</gloss>, add the lunar days <term>Tithi</term>, and divide by the cycle of the sky <gloss>9</gloss>
<gloss>64</gloss>... the result is the Ahargana <note>Total day count</note>. The day of the week is found from the remainder of the cycle. || ५ ||

<margin>
The months passed starting from Chaitra: 6.
Including the lunar days passed from the first day of the bright fortnight of Chaitra. || ६ ||
= In the place of zero is the day of Chaitra; this is the meaning of the cycle. ५
</margin>

**Calculation of the Mean Planet:** Subtracting the sky-mountain-parts from the heap of days, the Sun and Mercury <gloss>Hajaila</gloss>
The movement <gloss>150</gloss> sky-date-multiplied-heap <gloss>K-Anu</gloss> the minutes are self-manifested. <gloss>Thus the Sun.</gloss>

The difference between one's own location and the prime meridian: if West, add; if East, subtract. This is the correction for the mean motion <gloss>minus the center starting with Aries 9</gloss>. It should be understood that the movement is calculated according to the specific planet <gloss>thus the verse</gloss> and the local longitude <note>Sanskrit: *deshantara*; the longitudinal difference between the observer and the prime meridian</note> must be applied = 4.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical determination of planetary positions (mean motions) using the methods of the Grahalaghava. It focuses on the calculation of the Ahargana (total elapsed days) and the specific corrections for the five planets, the Sun, the Moon, and Rahu.</meta>

<margin>
From the planet's position, the <term>Ahargana</term><note>The total count of civil days elapsed from a fixed epoch.</note> is established in two ways. Divide by the constant, and the resulting parts are the degrees. The remainder is multiplied by sixty and divided again to find the minutes. These two results are subtracted from the Ahargana. Again, divide the Ahargana by two hundred and fifty (250); the result is in minutes. The remainder is multiplied by sixty and divided by three hundred. This result is subtracted from the previous position. This gives the "face" or starting position of Mercury. Here too, the minutes are western; if they are additive, they are divided by sixty and added to the degrees. The remainder becomes the signs. Then, multiply by the cycles and subtract the cycle-reduction, adding the <term>Kshepa</term><note>An additive constant or epochal position.</note>. Divide those signs by twelve to get the final signs and degrees. Others say these are the revolutions of the stars. This is the prior knowledge to be placed in the year by practitioners. ॥ 1 ॥
</margin>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical treatment of planetary positions, specifically focusing on the Sun and Moon (luminaries). The text belongs to the Grahalaghava, a seminal 16th-century Sanskrit astronomical treatise by Ganesha Daivajna.</meta>

<margin>
The method for calculating the planets at the desired time of sunrise. Then, the method for calculating the planets at sunset. In the aforementioned calculation of the observation, the effort should be made with diligence; this is the intended meaning.
</margin>

<margin>

<meta>This page continues the technical astronomical calculations regarding the "Three Problems" (time, place, and direction), specifically focusing on the determination of the Ascendant (Lagna) and the rising times of zodiac signs.</meta>

The degrees of the Sun's current position, adjusted for the precession of the equinoxes <note>original: "sayana"; the tropical longitude</note>, are subtracted from thirty to find the remaining degrees to be traversed. These are multiplied by the rising time of that sign <note>original: "svodaya"</note> and divided by thirty; the result is the remaining time of the current sign.

<gloss>measured in palas <note>a unit of time equal to 24 seconds</note></gloss>

## || Now, the Example of the Three Problems ||

|| The rising times at the equator <note>original: "lankodaya"</note> in breaths <note>original: "vighatika"</note> are: 278 for Aries, 298 for Taurus, and 333 for Gemini.

<margin>
With the local ascensional difference segments <note>original: "charakhandakas"</note>:
* The distance in yojanas <note>a traditional unit of distance</note> north of Avanti <note>modern Ujjain, the prime meridian of ancient Indian astronomy</note> should be multiplied by the shadow and divided by the radius. This is added or subtracted... <unclear>ink faded</unclear>... by 41.
Another method: The distance from Avanti... when the result is combined with five fingers, the distance for northern countries is 125.
</margin>

<margin>Local rising times</margin> These values (344, etc.) are placed in direct and reverse order. They are decreased or increased by half the ascensional difference <note>original: "charadala"</note> in direct and reverse order starting from Aries. From <gloss>Libra</gloss>, they are applied in reverse.

1. The remaining degrees of the Sun's position (adjusted for precession) are subtracted from thirty. The result is the remaining time. Similarly, the elapsed time is found from the elapsed degrees.

<margin>By the elements... 5</margin>
The elapsed time is to be calculated. The remaining time is subtracted from the desired time <note>original: "ishta-kala"</note> in ghatis and palas. 2. <gloss>From the subsequent signs</gloss>, their rising times are then subtracted. Multiply the remainder by thirty <note>original: "shad-gagana-guna"</note>...

<margin>The longitude of the Ascendant is found by adding the signs between the Sun and the Ascendant = 3</margin>
...the result in degrees and minutes is added to the preceding signs starting with Aries. This becomes the Ascendant <note>original: "Lagna"</note> when the precession <note>original: "ayanansha"</note> is subtracted. 3. If the desired time is less than the remaining time, multiply by thirty and divide by the local rising time to find the Sun's position in the Ascendant. The Sun's traversed portion added to the Ascendant's elapsed time...

<gloss>Divided by sixty, it becomes ghatis, etc.</gloss>
...added to the mean, becomes the desired time. 4. <gloss>In one sign</gloss>: If the Ascendant and the Sun are in the same sign, the difference in their degrees is multiplied by the rising time and divided by thirty to find the desired time. <gloss>The rising time of the tropical position</gloss> multiplied by <gloss>the desired time</gloss>... divided by the rising time... the tropical position is subtracted from the day and night <gloss>60</gloss> 60... becomes the Ascendant.

5. Regarding the celestial sphere: whether the planet is in the northern or southern hemisphere, in the signs beginning with Aries or Libra... <unclear>text breaks off</unclear>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical treatment of planetary positions, specifically focusing on the calculation of declination (the angular distance of a body north or south of the celestial equator).</meta>

The <term>declination</term> <note>original: "Kranti"</note> of a planet is to be calculated. From the <term>precession-corrected longitude</term> <note>original: "Sayana"</note>, one must determine the <term>sine-arc</term> <note>original: "Bhuja"</note>. Then, multiply the zodiacal signs by three and add the remaining degrees. Take a tenth part of this value. Then, the sum of the corresponding <term>sine-table segments</term> <note>original: "Khandas"</note> should be calculated. Following this, multiply the remaining portion of the current segment by the current sine-arc and divide by ten. Add this result to the previous sum. This total, when divided by ten, yields the declination in degrees and minutes. This method for finding declination is universal. Here is a verse in agreement with this rule: 1 2 =

The segments are: 10, 20, 37, 34, 20, 25, 16, 9, and 4. From the precession-corrected planet, within the 90-degree arc of the sine, sum these values. For the remainder, multiply and divide by ten to find the degrees.

<margin>
When the precession-corrected planet is in the Southern Hemisphere, the declination is South; otherwise, it is North.
Signs = 2
</margin>
Divided by 10, the degrees of <gloss>declination</gloss> are found. In the same way, according to one's own hemisphere: 11, 40, 80, 117, 151, 181, 206, 227, 236, 240.
From the precession-corrected position, the degrees are found. The remainder is divided by 10 and added to the sum.

<margin>
Rule for calculating a rough approximation of declination = 4
</margin>
<margin>
Rule for calculating declination without using segments = 3
</margin>
The declination will be in its own direction. By using the <gloss>precession-corrected</gloss> planet's sine-arc degrees, the <gloss>sum of segments</gloss> is found. The remaining <gloss>sine-arc</gloss> <gloss>segment</gloss> is added to the degrees, or <gloss>90 degrees</gloss> is the limit for declination. For easy calculation, multiply the precession-corrected sine-arc by 18 and subtract ten.

<margin>
Now, the rule for finding the sine-arc inversely from the declination = 2
Small segments = 2
</margin>
<gloss>Subtracting</gloss> from the base, divide by 72. The result, multiplied by twenty and divided by four and a half, gives the degrees of declination in its own hemisphere. From that half <gloss>declination</gloss>, multiply the remainder by 15. This, combined with the <gloss>segment</gloss> <gloss>sine-arc</gloss> count of 6, results in the degrees of the sine-arc.

# Regarding the construction of the sighting tube and the means of calculating the shadow and instrument parts for the Ascendant etc. 3

---

Between the base and the perpendicular, one should place a transverse wire; that is the hypotenuse. Thus a triangle is formed. A gnomon should be placed at the tip of the shadow. From the center of the point at its top, when the three strings are fixed, one should look at the planet in the sky through the tube attached to the string, following the line of the shadow. Alternatively, if one looks in the water, a central gnomon should be placed. Bring the center to the tip of the shadow; the tube should be placed on that string. A vessel of water should be placed at the position of the shadow's tip. One should look at the reflection of the planet in the water through the middle of the tube. This method is to be learned from a teacher. || 3

<vocab>
- **Hkarma (Visibility Correction):** A calculation to adjust the position of a planet to its visible rather than mathematical location.
- **Sphuṭaśarā (True Latitude):** The corrected celestial latitude of a body.
- **Krānti (Declination):** The angular distance of a body north or south of the celestial equator.
- **Bhujā / Koṭi / Karṇa:** The base, perpendicular, and hypotenuse of a right-angled triangle used in Indian trigonometry.
- **Yantra (Instrument):** Any astronomical tool used for measurement.
- **Nalikā (Tube):** A sighting tube used to isolate and observe a specific star or planet.
- **Siddhānta Rahasya:** The title of the work, meaning "The Secret of the Established Truths."
- **Chāyā (Shadow):** Used both for literal shadows and as a term for sines in trigonometry.
- **Kha-kha-adri:** A concrete number system (Bhuta-sankhya) representing 700.
</vocab>

# Now, the Chapter on the Lunar Eclipse

<note>original: "Atha Chandra-parva-adhikarah"</note>

Multiply the daily motion <gloss>of the planet</gloss> by the elapsed or remaining days and divide by sixty <note>original: "kharasa," a numerical code for 60</note>; the result, when subtracted from or added to the planet's position, gives the true position.

<margin>
In the southern hemisphere, the latitude decreases from the centers. || 3
</margin>

The time elapsed in ghatis <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> multiplied by the <gloss>daily</gloss> motion and divided by sixty gives the minutes of arc to be subtracted or added. 1. Thus, at the end of the lunar period <note>the full moon</note>, the Sun and Moon are calculated without the node <note>Rahu</note>.

<margin>
When the Sun is in the northern hemisphere, the latitude is north; in the southern hemisphere, the latitude is south.
</margin>

If the distance is within sixty-four degrees <note>original: "khansha"</note>, a lunar eclipse is possible. Multiply those degrees by eleven and divide by two hundred and eleven <gloss>to find the latitude</gloss>. Subtract the Sun's position from the Moon's; if the remainder is six signs...

<gloss>of the Sun</gloss> The diameter is calculated starting from two digits <note>angulas</note>. The motion multiplied by two and divided by ten <gloss>gives the diameter</gloss>; the body of the Moon is similarly determined. The motion of the Moon divided by seven hundred and forty-two...

<margin>In a small eclipse. || 2</margin>

<gloss>Lunar</gloss> The diameter is thus declared. The lunar motion minus seven hundred and sixteen, <gloss>divided</gloss> by twenty-two and thirty-two <note>original: "lochana-kara" and "dada"</note>, <gloss>gives the diameter of Rahu</gloss> <gloss>itself</gloss>. The Earth's shadow <note>original: "bhubha"</note> is the diameter of the Sun...

<margin>
Otherwise, it is always less than the diameter of Rahu. I shall speak of the obscuration here and nowhere else. || 2
</margin>

...minus the seventh part of the motion. What covers the Moon? <gloss>In a lunar eclipse</gloss>, the Earth's shadow is the obscurer and the Moon is the obscured. Calculate the half-sum of their diameters <term>Manaikya-khanda: the combined radii of the shadow and the Moon</term>.

Subtract the latitude from that; the result is the <gloss>magnitude of the eclipse</gloss>. When this obscured portion is subtracted from the diameter of the object to be eclipsed, the remainder is the clear portion. Add the half-sum of the diameters to the latitude, multiply by ten, and multiply by the obscured portion. The square root <gloss>root</gloss> of this, divided by sixty, gives the duration of the eclipse <note>original: "sthiti"</note> in ghatis and other units.

<summary>This page outlines the mathematical formulas for calculating a lunar eclipse, including the diameters of the Sun, Moon, and Earth's shadow, and the method for determining the duration and magnitude of the obscuration.</summary>
<keywords>lunar eclipse, Rahu, Earth's shadow, celestial latitude, Manaikya-khanda, ghati, astronomical calculation</keywords>

<margin>

<margin>
The difference between the two parts of the obscured portion, when halved, is the remainder. That, combined with the multiplier of the day, divided by two and five, and multiplied by seven. Then the square root, diminished by its own sixth part... the result is the duration of totality in hours and minutes <term>ghaṭikā: 24-minute units</term>.
---
The end of the lunar day, minus the duration of contact and totality, is the beginning of the eclipse. That same time, when added, is the release <note>end of the eclipse</note>. The moment of being covered is the immersion; the moment of being uncovered is the emergence.
---
The tropical Sun <term>sāyana: longitude including precession</term> increased or decreased by three signs is the celestial sphere. This is the meaning. 9.
The middle is from the end of the lunar day. At that time, the difference between the observer's view and the sky... the sine of the zenith distance... or it should be taken as given. 9.
At the inclination... of which the Sun's sign is multiplied... the state of being at the zenith... for the questioner... above the path of the immortals <note>the sky</note>... until the end of the inclination...

Here, from one's own... then by one's own duration, the time of release... should not be given, it is to be divided. Thus, by half of one's own duration... if the contact etc. occurs... then the direction of the release... this is the meaning. 4.
---
The fifth part of the degrees of inclination... the arc of the inclination is the remainder. Multiplied by thirty and divided by five, the result is degrees. The remainder multiplied by sixty and divided by five, the result is minutes. Thus, the degrees etc. are found. With the parts 157, here the degrees of the solstice should be brought. 4.
In the direction facing the middle of the planet, if there is no obscuration, what is the remainder? Therefore, there would be no deflection. 3.
</margin>

<margin>
There is no difference in the obscured part. The contact etc. which occurs in the Moon, when the remainder of the day has passed, or as in the case of the Sun at night... it should be in order. This is the calculation of the deflection for the western inclination. In the Moon, when the day is ending, the contact etc. occurs; then the difference from midday should be calculated. If it occurs in the remainder of the day, it is the western inclination. Similarly, if it occurs in the remainder of the night, the difference from midnight should be calculated. In the remainder of the night, it is the eastern inclination. If both deflections are in the same direction, they are added; if different, they are subtracted. 3.
---
Thus the deflection at the inclination is produced. But that deflection is called South/North... the contact etc. should be known. For the Moon, we calculate the direction of inclination... and that is from the west of the day. 10. Then at the end of the day, at night, there would be no inclination. 5.
</margin>

<term>Solar Eclipse (Sūryagrahaṇam)</term>: The alignment where the Moon passes between the Earth and the Sun.
<term>Signs (Rāśayaḥ)</term>: The twelve divisions of the zodiac (30 degrees each).
<term>Degrees (Aṃśāḥ)</term>: Units of angular measurement.
<term>Minutes (Kalā)</term>: Sub-divisions of a degree (60 minutes = 1 degree).
<term>Month-heap (Māsagaṇāḥ)</term>: The total number of lunar months elapsed since a fixed epoch.
<term>Fixed Constant (Dhruva)</term>: An astronomical constant used as a starting point for calculations.
<term>Intercalary Month (Adhimāsa)</term>: An extra month added to the lunar calendar to keep it in sync with the solar year.
<term>Refinement (Saṃskāra)</term>: Mathematical corrections applied to mean positions to find true positions.
<term>Digit (Aṅgula)</term>: A standard unit of length, here used to measure the diameter of the celestial disks.

Where an eclipse is first possible, one should first designate the starting point and then apply the "motion" <note>Sanskrit: *calana*, referring to the corrective movement or adjustment in longitude</note> as an addition. One must then observe if an eclipse occurs. If no eclipse is seen there, then a "month-motion" <note>adjustment for one synodic month</note> should be added. If the day-length is zero at the end of the lunar day <note>the moment of conjunction</note>, the planet is at the node.

If it does not occur then, a "fortnight-motion" should be subtracted; if still not, a "fortnight-motion" must be added. Even so, the conjunction is again designated at 3 <gloss>The lunar day etc. are 13; the planet is in the night</gloss> <gloss>At night, on the earth, during the lunar day</gloss> <gloss>The planet is at the end of the day or sunset = 4</gloss> <gloss>The lunar day etc. starting from the Sun = 2</gloss> <gloss>The lunar day and zodiac signs etc. = 2</gloss>

There may be a fraction of 10 | 50. The solar disk in digits <term>Angula: a traditional unit of measurement, here used for the apparent diameter of celestial bodies</term> is the divisor. The Moon should be increased by its own lunar day fraction. This is the calculation for the terrestrial months.

The terrestrial line is 50. From the previous half-day, the anomaly is considered to be 47 <gloss>Solar anomaly</gloss>. Having known this, one should state the remaining calculation for the planet at the lunar day node.

At the end of the conjunction, the time in *nadikas* <term>Nadika: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term> from midday or midnight—a quarter of those *nadikas* is subtracted from or added to the zodiac signs etc. to find the zenith distance degrees. These are refined by the declination degrees of the Sun to find the degrees of latitude. If these are multiplied by six or if the "arm" degrees are refined, and they are in the same direction, they are added; if in different directions, they are subtracted. These should be calculated seven times. If the "arm" degrees are not less than seven, then there is no solar eclipse. The remainder is added to its own value.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical instructions for calculating planetary positions and eclipse phenomena. It follows the establishment of the lunar month count (Masagana) and moves into the specific timing of conjunctions and the physical dimensions of the celestial bodies.</meta>

<margin>From the end of the lunar day <note>Tithi</note>, the positions of the Sun and Moon at that moment are adjusted by the remaining hours <note>Ghatis</note>. This means the resulting hours of the yoga <note>astronomical conjunction</note> occur after sunrise. || 1</margin>

<margin>Conjunction of stars.</margin> <margin>By the previously mentioned method.</margin>
The remaining hours of the star-conjunction are derived from the end of the lunar day. 19 <margin>If the Sun is eclipsed at sunrise, or the Moon is eclipsed at sunset during the night.</margin> If the remainder is two, the solar eclipse occurs; when combined with the midday constant, it determines the time before or after noon respectively. 20

->Thus ends the determination of the meaning of the Month-Count in the Siddhanta Rahasya composed by the illustrious Ganesha Daivajna.<-

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on the *Grahalaghava* (a 16th-century astronomical treatise). It focuses on calculating the rising and setting of planets, the length of day/night, and determining time using shadows and instruments.</meta>

# Chapter on Rising and Setting

First, one should calculate the planet's own ascensional difference <note>original: *charamāna*; the difference between the time of rising at the equator and at a specific latitude</note>. Then, from the latitude of the place and the planet's celestial latitude <note>original: *shara*</note>, one should determine if it is North or South. This is calculated using the shadow of the gnomon <note>original: *palabhā*</note> divided by 24 <note>original: *jinai*</note>. By applying the resulting value as an addition or subtraction to the previously calculated ascensional difference, the true ascensional difference is obtained. 5

-> That time, added to the remaining time of the sun's passage through its current sign and the rising times of the intervening signs, becomes the clear time of the planet's rising or setting. 1 <-

<margin>
To calculate the rising: from the given position, the time to be taken is the remaining time.
To calculate the setting: from the seventh sign from that position, the elapsed time is to be taken. 1
That time, added to the time of the planet at that moment, becomes clear. 2
Now, regarding the knowledge of whether a planet is visible or invisible at night and the calculation of the elapsed day, it is said "previously": if the planet given in the previous operation is less than the ascendant <note>the zodiac sign rising on the eastern horizon</note> at the time the shadow is measured, it is clear. If it is greater than the ascendant at that time, it is visible. If it is visible, the remaining time of the planet with its precession <note>original: *sayana*</note> should be calculated. Added to the elapsed time of the ascendant at that time, and added to the rising times of the signs between the planet and the ascendant, the planet's position becomes clear.
This is the elapsed time of the day. For the Moon, nine *palas* <note>units of time; 60 palas = 1 ghatika (24 minutes)</note> should be subtracted. To find the day from the clear shadow, from the total length of the day, the shadow is calculated through the "elevated" calculation. From this, the degrees of the instrument are determined. 3
</margin>

The time from the planet <gloss>15</gloss> is calculated from the opposite point <note>180 degrees away</note> plus six signs <gloss>||</gloss>. Added to the remaining time of the seventh sign from the sun and the sum of the middle rising times, it occurs in the month.

The planets at that time are clear. 24 For the Moon, however, it should be decreased by nine *palas* for every *ghatika* <note>24 minutes</note>; if increased by two *palas*, what would it be with the Moon of that time? 25 Thus ends the Chapter on Rising and Setting in the Illustrious *Grahalaghava*.

---

<gloss>Until that moment</gloss> what is the action? If the planet is less than the body <note>the ascendant</note> <gloss>5 with precession</gloss> it is at the end. <gloss>||</gloss> For the planet here, the remaining time of the planet <gloss>time of the celestial latitude</gloss> joined with the ascendant, the sum of the risings occurs. If it is the Moon, subtract nine *palas*. 1 24 <note>original: *jinā*</note> <gloss>||</gloss> Subtracting the celestial latitude from the fingers of the shadow.

<margin>
From the sign
</margin>
The clear ascensional difference <gloss>|| 5</gloss> having been refined, then from the planet's position at midday <gloss>1 || 5 planet shadow instrument etc.</gloss> the shadow etc. is perfected. If the planet is in the sky at night:

<margin>
For Mars and others, from the shadow
</margin>
In the elapsed time <gloss>15 || 55</gloss> the movement of the signs etc. is determined by the power of the instrument. 2 Or one may see the planet reflected in water and calculate its zenith distance <note>original: *driggoccha*</note>. That is the center of the reflection of the node. The shadow is produced by the zenith distance. Knowing this by inference, the time of night is found.

<margin>
By the power of the instrument, the degrees of the planet are known through the observation with the instrument. From those, the time is known; from the shadow and instrument, the elapsed day is known. 2
Then, the center of the reflection in the water from the plumb line should be measured in fingers <note>original: *angula*</note>. That difference multiplied by twelve and divided by the zenith distance gives the shadow in fingers. 3
It is said "having known": having inferred the elapsed *ghatikas*, the ascendant of that time is calculated. From that, the length of the day and the shadow etc. are calculated using the clear ascensional difference. Then, by the reverse method, the elapsed time of the day is found.

The difference of two latitudes in the same direction and the sum of those in different directions should be calculated. By doing this, the distance between the centers of the planets in digits and other units is found. If this distance is less than half the sum of their diameters, it is a "piercing" conjunction.

In that case, should the parallax and other corrections be applied as in a solar eclipse? No, because the contact and other phases are not perceived here. Therefore, why perform the heavy calculations of parallax? It is only as previously described for the solar eclipse. = 2

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of astronomical "Pāta" (the alignment of the Sun and Moon's declination). It focuses on calculating the exact moments of Vyatīpāta and Vaidhṛti, two significant astrological phenomena, using the "Sāyana" (tropical) positions of the celestial bodies.</meta>

# On the Knowledge of the Alignment of Declination

The alignment of the declination <note>original: *krānti-sāmya*</note> of the Moon and the Sun is known as *Pāta*. To determine this, one must calculate the elapsed and remaining time. Multiply the daily motion by nine and divide... the result is thirteen (130). Similarly, the twenty-seven yogas <note>astronomical divisions of the ecliptic</note> are to be decreased or increased by the ghatis <note>units of time equal to 24 minutes</note>. In this way, the elapsed and remaining time of the yoga should be recorded in ghatis and minutes. When the sum of the positions equals five degrees, the *Pāta* occurs. Where the sum is twenty-seven, it is called the *Vyatīpāta*. The time elapsed or remaining is multiplied by the remaining ghatis and divided by five to find the precise time. At this time, Rahu <note>the ascending node</note> must be added to the Sun to make it "Sāyana" <note>tropical, adjusted for precession</note>.

---

-> This is not 2. When the ghatis are equal to the degrees of the solstice, and the solar/lunar disks are aligned at 130, the yoga of the ocean is passed. 1 <-

**The *Pāta* is known as *Vyatīpāta* [when the Sun and Moon are in different hemispheres but have equal declination] and *Vaidhṛti* otherwise. The ghatis should always be multiplied by the constant to be made clear: 65. || 1 ||**

**Here, the nodes and the Sun must be made tropical <note>original: *sāyana*</note>. <gloss>1</gloss> If they are in the same hemisphere, the sum of their degrees is taken; if in different hemispheres, the difference is the *Pāta*.**

**If the degrees are less than 55, then the *Pāta* is certain. If they are greater, there is doubt. This doubt regarding the Sun is resolved by the following method. || 2 ||**

The values are 0, 0, 12, 6, 18, 5. For the positions of the Sun with the node, the segments are 3, 8, 1, 12, 6, 20, 22. In case of difference, the additive factors <note>original: *kṣepa*</note> are 6, 10, etc. By using the 55 divisions, the sum of the segments is calculated. The remaining degrees are multiplied by the current segment and added to the sum to find the *Sandhi* <note>the junction or threshold value</note>. || 3 ||

If the declination is equal at the *Sandhi*, then the *Pāta* exists. If the degrees of the Sun's arm <note>original: *bhujāṃśa* - the distance from the nearest equinox</note> are greater than the *Sandhi*, the difference between them represents the difference in declination between the Sun and Moon. || 4 || In an even or odd quadrant, if the Sun is in the same or different hemisphere, the *Pāta* is either past or yet to come. If the hemispheres are different, and the degrees of the Sun's arm are smaller than the calculated value, the position is as follows. || 4 ||

<margin>
Knowledge of the existence or non-existence of the *Pāta* in the world:
When the Sun and Moon are in the same hemisphere, a *Pāta* occurs. If they are in different hemispheres, one must calculate the degrees of the tropical Sun's "arm" <note>distance from the equinox</note>. If those degrees are less than 55, the *Pāta* occurs. If the degrees are greater than 55, there is doubt whether it occurs or not. We resolve that doubt through the calculation of the *Sandhi* (junction) values. || 3 ||

Now, the characteristics of whether the *Pāta* has passed or is coming:
If the Sun with the node is in an even or odd quadrant respectively, the *Pāta* has passed. This means: if the Sun is in the same hemisphere and in an even quadrant, or in a different hemisphere and an odd quadrant, the *Pāta* is "gone" (past). If the opposite is true, the *Pāta* is "to come."
</margin>

<margin>
Regarding the calculation:
When the Sun and Moon are in the same quadrant, the segments starting from zero are used. If they are in different quadrants, the segments starting from 12 are used. The tropical Sun's longitude is divided into five-degree segments. The sum of these segments is calculated. The remaining degrees are multiplied by the next segment and added to the sum plus the additive factor <note>kṣepa</note> to find the *Sandhi*. To know if the *Pāta* exists: if the Sun's degrees are less than the *Sandhi*, there is equality of declination. If they are greater, there is no equality, but the difference between the Sun's degrees and the *Sandhi* shows the actual difference in declination between the Sun and Moon. || 4 ||
</margin>

<gloss>
If the Sun is in an even quadrant but in a different hemisphere, the *Pāta* is occurring. Regarding the Sun's position: if the hemispheres are different and the Sun's tropical degrees are very small, the quadrant position must be carefully observed. If the Sun is in an even quadrant, treat it as odd; if in an odd quadrant, treat it as even. After determining this reversal, use the rule "in even or odd quadrants" to find if the *Pāta* is past or future. || 2 ||
</gloss>

<note>Technical terms used: Vyatīpāta and Vaidhṛti (specific astronomical alignments), Krānti-sāmya (equality of declination), Sāyana (tropical/with precession), Bhujāṃśa (arc of the longitude), Sandhi (calculated threshold).</note>

<header>Chapter 18</header>

<margin>
Chapter 18

Now, the calculation of the five-fold divisions of the latitude <note>original: "shara"</note>. The degrees of the Sun's arm <note>the sine of the solar longitude</note> should be calculated. One-fifth of these degrees are equal to the latitude units. The sum of these should be multiplied by the "arm" and divided by eight; when added to the fifth part, the result is the latitude.
</margin>

Now, the calculation of the latitude <note>shara</note> suitable for the correction of declination <note>kranti</note>. Regarding the "ten-fold" method: take the tenth part of the degrees of the Sun's arm. By these tenths of the Sun's arm <gloss>the Sun</gloss>, the divisor is determined. These are the tenth-parts, such as 5, etc. By the difference of the desired positions found previously, the multipliers are determined. The result of the division is the clear <term>Spashta: the corrected or "true" value</term> latitude. Because the difference is small, the latitude is calculated again for accuracy. For the sake of the "Great Correction" <note>Man-samskara</note>, the latitude becomes clear. = 2

# The difference in the signs is appropriate. 5. Five-fold are the oceans; five-fold are the waters; two are four-fold; the earth, the void, and the sky are the arrows. 4 | 4 |

4 | 4 | 4 | 4 | 3 | 3 | 3 | 3 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 1 | 0 | 0. By one's own fire <note>the number 3</note> and the day, the remaining portions are equal to the degrees of the Sun's arm.

# By the addition of the degrees, the God of Love <note>the number 13</note> is reached. 6. In the tenth-parts of the Sun's arm, the divisors are the Sun, the Manu, and the fixed points.

22 | 22 | 24 | 28 | 37 | 56 | 70 | 100 | 127. The latitude divisors are the ancient mountains and the Rama <note>the number 3</note>; the void and the beginning are doubled. Here:

<margin>
Furthermore, regarding the inversion: among the numbers placed in order, the middle numbers are inverted. They are considered to be in the opposite direction. This means if they were North, they become South; if South, they become North. Here also, the declination numbers are placed in six positions and processed by the zodiac signs. If the directions are the same, they are added; if different, they are subtracted. Thus processed, they are divided by thirteen to find the declination. Thus, the numbers become much clearer. = 9
</margin>

# By subtraction here, the corrected value becomes clear through the Great Correction. 7. Four-fold are the earth and the mountains; the ten elephants of the directions are taken.

# The solar disk and the ten directions are the positions; three are the declination numbers. 20 | 20 | 20 | 20 | 19 | 19 | 19 | 18 | 16 | 14

12 | 12 | 10 | 8 | 7 | 5 | 3 | 1. In order, the remainder of the other is equal to the degrees of the Sun's arm. 8. In order:

<margin>
Now he speaks of the declination numbers. "Four-fold," etc. One-fifth of the degrees of the arm of the <term>Sayana: the tropical longitude (including precession)</term> Sun becomes the past declination number. The remaining arm is to be placed for the future. This will be used further on. = 5

18
</margin>

The numbers of the latitude and declination mentioned are the six numbers to be placed for the past and future respectively. When the node <note>the intersection of the orbit and the ecliptic</note> is even, they are added; these are the degrees of precession <note>ayanansha</note>. 9. And so on, as if in play <note>Lilamaya</note>. Here:

---

Now, the sexagesimal conversion <note>making it based on 60</note> of the latitude and declination numbers and their correction. He says "in order." In the arena of calculation, the latitude numbers and the nine declination numbers are placed in sequence. From the current position, six numbers are placed for the past and future. If the node is past, six numbers are placed from the past; if the node is future, six numbers are placed from the future. Thus, for the latitude numbers: at the past node, the six past numbers; at the future node, the future numbers. In this way, when the Sun is in an even quadrant, the numbers are added. In an even quadrant, the latitude numbers are equal. In an odd quadrant, at the past node, the future numbers are used, and vice versa. These numbers are to be known as the degrees of precession <note>ayanansha</note>. During the Northern transit <note>Uttarayana</note>, the declination numbers are North; during the Southern transit <note>Dakshinayana</note>, they are South. Thus, according to the quadrant and the tropical <note>Sayana</note> position, the South direction is understood. 2

<note>Technical Vocabulary: **Kranti** (Declination), **Shara** (Celestial Latitude), **Ravibhujamsha** (Degrees of the Sun's "arm" or sine), **Sayana** (Tropical longitude including precession), **Samskara** (Correction/Processing), **Bhogyamsha** (Degrees yet to be traversed), **Shadanka** (The six-digit calculation method).</note>

It emphasizes the "five-fold" and "ten-fold" methods and explains how to adjust these values based on the Sun's tropical position and the lunar nodes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical calculations for the Pātādhikāra (Chapter on the Nodes/Declination Parity) from the Grahalāghava. It specifically addresses the timing of the "middle of the Pāta" (the moment of exact declination equality) and the subsequent calculation of the Moon's position for that instant.</meta>

<header>The calculation for the middle of the Pāta is stated: Those digits previously established <note>referring to the mathematical constants used in the Grahalāghava system</note> are to be refined. The remaining degrees are divided by five, then decreased by one and so on; this is known as the "Laghusanjna" (the lesser multiplier). If the digits there are to be considered, whatever is stated must be divided by the divisor...</header>

<margin>
Furthermore, if some digits are to be subtracted from the arrows <note>Sanskrit: 'shara', referring to the sine of latitude or specific mathematical offsets</note>, then those digits must be refined. From them, the entire calculation for the middle of the Pāta is derived. He states the calculation for the duration: twelve <note>original: 'yamarka' (12)</note> ghatikas <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> are divided by the clear motion. The result is the duration before and after the middle of the Pāta. During these ghatikas, before the middle, the Pāta begins; afterwards, it concludes. In the middle ghatikas between the beginning and the end, acts such as bathing, giving charity, chanting (japa), and fire sacrifices (havana) yield infinite merit. However, marriages and similar auspicious events are forbidden. This is the purpose.
</margin>

# The maximum digits, refined in order by the Moon's motion, become clearer through the division of the declination.

<margin>
Multiplied by three and also by the ghatikas of the asterism (nakshatra) that have passed, then divided by three. The result is the time in ghatikas. This occurs in the middle of the Pāta. This is the alternative explanation for verses eleven and twelve.
</margin>

### 10. The previously established remaining degrees, divided by five, and increased by one (6), are called the "Laghusanjna" (lesser multipliers). The first clear...
### 11. ...digit is multiplied by the "Laghu." By that, they are increased or decreased respectively. Those digits, in the absence of others, are combined with the Moon (6)...
### 12. ...multiplied by the "Laghu," then tripled, and multiplied by the nakshatra-ghatikas. This is divided by the elapsed and remaining ghatikas. In the middle (12), the digits are sometimes here...
### 13. ...to be refined. The digits in front of them are multiplied by the non-lunar factor. The duration is twelve (12) ghatikas before and after the middle of the Pāta.
### 14. Now, he describes the knowledge of the Moon at the time of equal declination: The Sun, increased by six signs (12) <note>original: 'shadarka'</note>, becomes the tropical (Saayana) Aries. At that time, the ghatikas are equal to the minutes of the motion. The motion of the Sun and Moon, divided by 800, gives the degrees of travel. This is for the sake of perceiving the equality of declination.

->Thus ends the Chapter on the Nodes (Pātādhikāra) in the Shri Grahalāghava.<-

### The months, increased by their own twelve, are the days for the duration...

---

Now, to remove the confusion of the students and to provide knowledge of the possibility of declination parity, he explains the knowledge of the Moon: "By six and twelve..." In this context, at the middle of the Pāta, the Sun plus six signs (12) becomes the tropical (Saayana) Sun. In the case of Vyatīpāta <note>a specific astronomical yoga where the Sun and Moon have equal but opposite declinations</note>, the Sun plus six signs is the tropical Moon. In the case of Vaidhṛti <note>where declinations are equal and in the same direction</note>, the first digit is the tropical Moon. Furthermore, from all the elapsed time, the minutes of the motion at the middle of the Pāta are derived. In a past Pāta, they are subtracted; in a future Pāta, they are added. The sum of the elapsed ghatikas of the current nakshatra divided by 800 gives the portions of the Moon's motion. By those portions of motion, the Moon is to be moved. This was <unclear>stated to remove confusion</unclear>... for the conviction of the students.

<summary>This page concludes the Pātādhikāra chapter of the Grahalāghava, detailing the mathematical procedures for finding the exact moment of declination parity (Pāta) and the Moon's position at that time. It emphasizes the spiritual significance of this window of time for religious rites while prohibiting social ceremonies like marriage.</summary>

The void <note>zero</note> named seven 0/7/28, multiplied by the cycle, is joined with the "eight-mountains" <term>ghatikas</term> (48). 7 being purified, multiplied by two and the <term>Dhurjatis</term><note>A name for Shiva, representing the number 11</note> (2/11), and joined with the months, it becomes the auspicious <term>Bhastvaka</term> 2. <margin>Book 20</margin>
The Era of King Vikrama <note>original: "Vikramesha," referring to the Vikrama Samvat calendar starting 57 BCE</note> is 1540. From the difference of the base calculation <note>term: "pinda" (base quantity or sum)</note> of sixty ghatis <note>term: "ghati" (a unit of time equal to 24 minutes)</note>, from the celestial base and the dual base 2;17 <unclear>glo vim</unclear>... from that island's light... known as ten, joined with the treasures 3;12. Starting with the day of the week, the heard qualities of the earth <note>note: "kuguna" is a numerical code for 31</note> are 13;150. In the base of the Lord's ghati 2;11, for those in sequence, the total planetary cycles <note>term: "yuganka"</note> for each month are 84. The seconds should be subtracted by 4.

Thus, in the example from the Chapter on Determining the Almanac <note>original: "Panchanga-grahana-adhikara"</note> in the *Grahalaghava* <note>note: A famous astronomical treatise written by Ganesha Daivajna in 1520 CE</note>: 1542. Subtract the Shaka era <note>note: The Shaka calendar starts in 78 CE</note> from this; then from the remaining cycles of the Sun, the remainder is diminished by the months starting with Chaitra. Separately from this, combined with the cycles of the planets and the intercalary months <note>term: "adhimasa" (extra months added to lunar calendars to align with the solar year)</note> 1. Multiplied by thirty and subtracted by the lunar days <note>term: "tithi"</note>, the result is placed separately <gloss>joined</gloss>. Subtracted by the days made by the sixty-four <margin>passed watches of the day</margin>, the result is the "heap of days" <note>term: "ahargana" (the number of elapsed days from a fixed epoch)</note>. From this, the day of the week is known. From the fixed constants multiplied by the cycles, and the multipliers of the days, the planets are diminished. If here the years are small, or if one <gloss>the best of days</gloss> 2, all are very advanced. Whatever cycles were made <gloss>gone to the end</gloss> without the victories of the bow <note>note: "Dhanurjaya" likely refers to a specific rival or a previous calculation method</note>, they do not climb the mountain of great pride and sorrow. Here, the entire statement of the Siddhanta <note>term: "Siddhanta" (established astronomical system)</note> has been simplified by me, having set aside the methods of Dhanurjaya. Let the experts not find fault in me; why should the dull-witted be struck by weapons? 1. Not in a distant village, but in that region, he is praised by the songs of disciples and others: Keshav, the king of the Kaushika lineage, the knower of the meaning of all scriptures, is honored.

->Since my intellect was born from the study of these true scriptures, pride is not appropriate for me. = 2<-

<meta>This page concludes the astronomical treatise, following the calculations for the five limbs of the calendar (Panchanga). The text transitions from technical instruction to a traditional eulogy of the mathematical sciences.</meta>

<margin>
Book 2
</margin>

Through the portion of knowledge obtained here by the grace of the Lotus-born <note>The god Brahma</note>, Ganesha has clearly composed this work in various meters and with simplified methods. 2. Other sciences are fruitless; they lead only to disputes. In contrast, the Sun and Moon of the entire science of Astral Science <term>Jyotisha: the combined study of astronomy and astrology</term> are proven through instruments. 3. Just as the crest sits upon the heads of peacocks, and as the jewels reside in the heads of serpents, so does Mathematics <term>Ganita</term> stand at the crown of all the sciences of the Vedic limbs. 4. A knower of the fundamental principles <term>Siddhanta</term> destroys the sins of ten days; a knower of the instructional treatises <term>Tantra</term> destroys the sins of three days; a knower of the concise astronomical manuals <term>Karana</term> destroys the sins of a day and night. Conversely, one who points out errors in these without cause generates great sin. 5. That twice-born scholar who knows this work entirely, both by its text and its meaning, shall be the first to be fed at a funeral feast <note>Shraddha: a high honor for a Brahmin</note>, honored as one who purifies the assembly. 6. Even those who have taken to the forest, possessing nothing and free from ego, even they inquire of the expert in the movements of the stars. 7. Thus ends the Chapter on the Calculation of the Five Limbs of the Calendar in the **Secret of the Established Principles, composed by the venerable Ganesha the Astrologer, the author of the Grahalaghava. This book was completed in the Year 1859 <note>Vikrama Samvat, approx. 1802 CE</note>, in the month of Phalguna, during the bright fortnight, on the 9th day, a Wednesday.**

# Shri Raghunatha Temple Manuscript Library,
->JAMMU<-

---

**No.** 868-D <note>original: ८६८-घ; the letter 'D' or 'Gha' likely refers to a specific sub-section of the collection</note>

# Shri Raghunatha Temple Manuscript Library, JAMMU

# Shri Raghunatha Temple MSS. Library,
## JAMMU

---

**No.** 868.D <note>original: ८६८.घ; the letter 'D' indicates a sub-classification in the library's numbering system</note>

**Title** *The Light of the Planets* <note>original: ग्रहलाघव: (Grahalaghava); a famous 16th-century astronomical treatise used for calculating planetary positions without complex trigonometry</note>

**Author** Ganesha the Astrologer <note>original: गणेशदैवज्ञः (Ganesha Daivagna); a renowned mathematician and astronomer from Nandigaon</note>

**Extent** 21 <note>Refers to the number of folios or leaves in the manuscript</note> **Age** 1856 <note>Likely the date of the manuscript's copying or acquisition in the Vikram Samvat calendar, corresponding to approximately 1799–1800 CE</note>

**Subject** Astronomy/Astrology. <term>Complete</term> <note>original: ज्योतिषम् (Jyotish). In this context, it refers to the traditional Indian science of time-keeping and celestial observation. 'Sampurnam' indicates the text is not a fragment.</note>
